Mass concentration in a spatially inhomogeneous coagulation model with fast sedimentation
Iulia Cristian, Juan J. L. Velázquez

Abstract
We study a spatially inhomogeneous coagulation model that contains a transport term in the spatial variable. The transport term models the vertical motion of particles due to gravity, thereby incorporating their fall into the dynamics. Local existence of mass-conserving solutions for a class of coagulation rates for which in the spatially homogeneous case instantaneous gelation (i.e., instantaneous loss of mass) occurs has been proved in [ 9 ] . In order to obtain some insight into how to prove global existence of solutions, we allow a fast sedimentation speed. For very fast sedimentation speed, we rigorously prove that solutions converge to a Dirac measure in the space variable. We also formally obtain in the limit a one-dimensional coagulation equation with diagonal kernel, i.e., only particles of the same size interact. This provides a physical intuition on how coagulation models with a diagonal kernel emerge.



Keywords:
inhomogeneous coagulation equations, mass-conserving solutions, sum-type kernels, diagonal kernel, rain formation

1
Introduction

1.1
Background

It is known that gelation (loss of mass) occurs for the standard one-dimensional coagulation model,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}f(v,\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{(0,v)}K(v-w,w)f(v-w,\tau)f(w,\tau)\textup{d}w-\int_{(0,\infty)}K(v,w)f(v,\tau)f(w,\tau)\textup{d}w,

when the coagulation kernel behaves like a power law of homogeneity
\gamma>1
, see
[
15
,
21
,
13
,
18
]
. In addition, for sum kernels of the form

\displaystyle K(v,w)=v^{\gamma}+w^{\gamma},\quad\gamma>1,

(1.1)

gelation happens instantaneously and one can prove that solutions belonging to
L^{1}
do not exist, see
[
1
,
2
,
6
,
12
]
. Nonetheless, there are some results concerning the existence of solutions for sum-type kernels as in (
1.1
) in the spatially inhomogeneous case,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}f(x,v,\tau)+v^{\alpha}\partial_{x}f(x,v,\tau)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{(0,v)}K(v-w,w)f(x,v-w,\tau)f(x,w,\tau)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle-\int_{(0,\infty)}K(v,w)f(x,v,\tau)f(x,w,\tau)\textup{d}w

(1.2)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{1}[f](x,v,\tau)-\mathbb{K}_{2}[f](x,v,\tau)=:\mathbb{K}[f](x,v,\tau),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.

More precisely, when
\gamma<\alpha+1,\alpha\geq 0
, existence of solutions (which may not preserve mass) for a discrete version of (
1.1
) with a coagulation kernel as in (
1.1
) was proved in
[
19
]
, while existence of mass-conserving solutions for short times for the model (
1.1
) when
\gamma<\alpha+1,\alpha\in(0,1)
was proved in
[
9
]
. It is also worth mentioning that the relevance of the model in (
1.1
) with coagulation kernels of homogeneity
\gamma>1
stems from the fact that it was first introduced in order to describe the formation of rain or the behavior of air bubbles in water, see
[
20
,
27
,
17
,
29
]
. In this case, the coagulation kernel has the form

\displaystyle K(v,w)=|v^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha}|(v^{\frac{1}{3}}+w^{\frac{1}{3}})^{2}.

(1.3)

The main idea in
[
9
]
in order to prove existence of mass-conserving solutions for the model (
1.1
) is that the coagulation operator
\mathbb{K}[f]
gives a negligible contribution for sufficiently small times. It is thus natural to ask what is the time range for which mass-conserving solutions exist if we work with a model of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)+v^{\alpha}\partial_{x}f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)=\epsilon\mathbb{K}[f_{\epsilon}](x,v,\tau),

(1.4)

for some sufficiently small
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and when
K
is as in (
1.1
) or (
1.3
). We expect that this modification of our model will offer clues on how to prove global in time existence of mass-conserving solutions. The main goal of this paper is thus to prove that mass-conserving solutions of (
1.4
) exist for times of order
\mathcal{O}(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}})
. While kernels of homogeneity
\gamma\leq 1
are well-studied in the mathematical literature, see for example
[
14
,
16
,
28
]
, this is the first result of existence of mass-conserving solutions involving kernels of homogeneity
\gamma>1
, with the exceptions being the product kernel
[
25
,
31
]
and our previous result in
[
9
]
.

Notice also that since we can rescale the time
\tau\rightarrow\epsilon\tau
, (
1.4
) is equivalent to the following form.

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{x}f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)=\mathbb{K}[f_{\epsilon}](x,v,\tau).

(1.5)

1.2
Connection with coagulation models with diagonal kernel

The first question is whether the proof in
[
9
]
can be reproduced in order to extend the existence result for the model (
1.4
). It was argued in
[
8
, Section 6.4]
that the steps to tackle
local in time existence cannot be reproduced in order to prove global existence of solutions (or even existence for times of order one) for the model (
1.4
). As such, a new strategy needs to be adopted.

Different forms of solutions for the model (
1.4
) have then been considered in
[
8
, Section 6.5]
(such as polynomial decaying solutions, Dirac measure solutions, and Dirac measure solutions with sufficiently fast decay in the
v
variable) and it has been noticed at a formal level that the solutions can be expected to portray a self-similar behavior of the form
f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)\approx\frac{1}{\tau}S_{\epsilon}\big(\frac{x}{\tau},v\big)
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
.

Motivated by the computations in
[
8
]
, we make the following rescaling for solutions of (
1.1
).

\displaystyle f(x,v,\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau+1}H(y,v,t),\quad y:=\frac{x}{\tau+1},\quad t:=\ln(\tau+1),

(1.6)

or, in other words, we have that

\displaystyle H(y,v,t)=e^{t}f(x,v,\tau),\quad x:=e^{t}y,\quad\tau:=e^{t}-1.

(1.7)

Then
H
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H(y,v,t)+\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H(y,v,t)]=\mathbb{K}[H](y,v,t).

(1.8)

Since the rain kernel (
1.3
) vanishes on the diagonal
\{v=w\}
, it is straightforward to check that a steady state of the model (
1.8
) is given by
G(v)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
.

Ansatz 1.1
.

We thus make the following ansatz in the case when the coagulation kernel in (
1.8
) is
K_{\epsilon}(v,w)=\epsilon(v^{\gamma}+w^{\gamma})
, for some
\epsilon<1
small. For large times, solutions of the model (
1.8
) can be expected to concentrate around
|y-v^{\alpha}|\ll 1
. Notice however that
G(v)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
are not steady states of (
1.8
) in this case. Nonetheless, our assumptions are motivated by the fact that
K_{\epsilon}
and the rain kernel (
1.3
) both behave like sum-type kernels with a small contribution on the diagonal
\{v=v^{\prime}\}
.

We thus formally assume
H
is of the form

\displaystyle H(y,v,t)=G(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha}).

(1.9)

We integrate the coagulation operator
\mathbb{K}
in the
y
variable. For the loss term
\mathbb{K}_{2}
in (
1.1
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{2}[H](y,v,t)\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}K(v,w)G(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})G(w,t)\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\textup{d}w\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,\infty)}K(v,w)G(v,t)G(w,t)\delta(w^{\alpha}-v^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{1-\alpha}}{\alpha}\int_{(0,\infty)}(v^{\gamma}+w^{\gamma})G(v,t)G(w,t)\delta(w-v)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\alpha}v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}(G(v,t))^{2}.

For the gain term
\mathbb{K}_{1}
in (
1.1
), it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{1}[H](y,v,t)\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,v)}K(v-w,w)G(v-w,t)\delta(y-(v-w)^{\alpha})G(w,t)\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\textup{d}w\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{(0,v)}K(v-w,w)G(v-w,t)G(w,t)\delta((v-w)^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\bigg(\frac{v}{2}\bigg)^{1-\alpha}\int_{(0,v)}((v-w)^{\gamma}+w^{\gamma})G(v-w,t)G(w,t)\delta(w-\frac{v}{2})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\bigg(\frac{v}{2}\bigg)^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\bigg(G\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}.

(1.10)

Thus,
G
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G(v,t)=\frac{2}{\alpha}\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{v}{2}\bigg)^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\bigg(G(\frac{v}{2},t)\bigg)^{2}-v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}(G(v,t))^{2}\bigg].

(1.11)

Remark 1.2
.

Thus,
G
satisfies a coagulation equation in which the coagulation kernel is a diagonal kernel of the form
v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\delta(v-w)
multiplied by some constant. Notice that this means the kernel has homogeneity
\gamma-\alpha
and thus the existence of solutions for this type of equation is well-known and has been proved in
[
26
,
24
,
23
]
when
\gamma-\alpha<1
.

1.3
Self-consistent argument for behavior of solutions as
t\rightarrow\infty

Equation (
1.11
) suggests (at least formally) that the function
G(v,t):=\int H(y,v,t)\textup{d}y
, with
H
as in (
1.8
), satisfies a one-dimensional
coagulation model with a diagonal kernel of homogeneity
\gamma-\alpha<1
. We denote by
[\cdot]
the dimensional properties of a quantity. We analyze the scaling properties of mass-conserving solutions to (
1.11
). Mass-conservation
\int vG(v,t)\textup{d}v\equiv constant
implies that

\displaystyle[G][v]^{2}=1.

(1.12)

From (
1.11
), we further deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{[G]}{[t]}=[v]^{\gamma-\alpha+1}[G]^{2}.

(1.13)

From (
1.12
) and (
1.13
), we have that

\displaystyle[G]=\frac{1}{[v]^{\gamma-\alpha+1}[t]}\textup{ and }[v]=[t]^{\frac{1}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}}.

With Ansatz
1.1
, we have that
y
should rescale as
v^{\alpha}
and thus

\displaystyle[y]=[v]^{\alpha}=[t]^{\frac{\alpha}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}}.

Since we also want mass-conservation
\int\int vH(y,v,\tau)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y\equiv constant
, then the long-time behavior of solutions
H
in (
1.8
) is described by
g
, with
g
having the following form.

\displaystyle H(y,v,t)=\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{2+\alpha}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}}}g(Y,V,s),\textup{ with }Y:=\frac{y}{t^{\frac{\alpha}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}}},\quad V:=\frac{v}{t^{\frac{1}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}}},\quad s=\ln t.

Denoting by
\beta:=\frac{1}{1-(\gamma-\alpha)}
, we obtain that
g
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{\beta(2+\alpha)+1}}

\displaystyle\big[\partial_{s}g(Y,V,s)-\beta(2+\alpha)g-\beta V\partial_{V}g-\alpha\beta Y\partial_{Y}g\big]+\tau^{-\beta(2+\alpha)}\partial_{Y}[(V^{\alpha}-Y)g]=\frac{\tau^{\beta(\gamma+1)}}{\tau^{2\beta(2+\alpha)}}\mathbb{K}[g]

or alternatively since
\beta(\gamma-\alpha-1)+1=0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\partial_{s}g(Y,V,s)-\beta(2+\alpha)g-\beta V\partial_{V}g-\alpha\beta Y\partial_{Y}g+e^{s}\partial_{Y}[(V^{\alpha}-Y)g]=\mathbb{K}[g](Y,V,s),

where we remember that we are interested in the behavior for large times.

Notice that the transport term
e^{s}\partial_{Y}[(V^{\alpha}-Y)g]
can be expected to give a large contribution as
s\rightarrow\infty
. This justifies the study of the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(x,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{x}[(v^{\alpha}-x)H_{\epsilon}(x,v,t)]=\mathbb{K}[H_{\epsilon}](x,v,t)

as a first natural step in order to study the long-time behavior of solutions to (
1.1
).

1.4
Connection with previous results for spatially inhomogeneous coagulation models

Under these conditions, the natural approach in order to extend the existence result of mass-conserving solutions is thus to analyze the following rescaled version of the model (
1.4
). Let
f_{\epsilon}
be a solution of (
1.4
). Take
H_{\epsilon}
to be such that

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau+1}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t),\quad y:=\frac{x}{\tau+1},\quad t:=\epsilon\ln(\tau+1),

(1.14)

or, in other words, we have that

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)=e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}f_{\epsilon}(x,v,\tau),\quad x:=e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}y,\quad\tau:=e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}-1.

(1.15)

Then
H_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]=\mathbb{K}[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,t).

(1.16)

We analyze equation (
1.16
) in more detail. We first remember the existence result for mass-conserving solutions in
[
9
]
.

Theorem 1.3

(Local existence of mass-conserving solutions for small times)
.

Let
K
be as in (
1.1
) or (
1.3
) with
\alpha\in(0,1),\gamma\in[0,1+\alpha)
. Let
T>0
be sufficiently small. Let
f_{\textup{in}}\in\textup{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})
such that

\displaystyle f_{\textup{in}}(x,v)\leq\frac{A}{1+|x|^{m}+v^{p}},

for some
A>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
v\in(0,\infty)
, and where
m=\alpha p
,
m
even. Then there exists a mass-conserving solution
f
for the equation (
1.4
).

In our case, this translates to

f_{\epsilon}
satisfying (
1.4
)

H_{\epsilon}
satisfying (
1.16
)

existence for
\tau\in[0,T]
, with
T
small

existence for
t\in[0,\epsilon\ln(T+1)]
, with
T
small

Table 1
:
Connection between the two equations

Remark 1.4
.

Notice that the existence from the previous paper translates for our equation (
1.16
) to a time of existence of order
\epsilon
. This means that as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
(so in the limit) the time of existence goes to zero. Thus, we prove in this paper existence of mass-conserving solutions for equation (
1.16
) for a time of order one which is independent of
\epsilon
. This will imply existence of mass-conserving solutions for times of order
\mathcal{O}(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}})
to the equation (
1.4
).

There is a simple heuristic argument that explains why mass-conserving solutions to equation (
1.16
) should exist. Assuming that the coagulation operator gives a negligible contribution, we look at the following PDE (for some suitable test function
\varphi
).

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\int H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\varphi(y,v,t)\textup{d}y\textup{d}v-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\partial_{y}\varphi(y,v,t)\textup{d}y\textup{d}v

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,0)

\displaystyle=H_{\textup{in}}(y,v),

for which we know the limiting behavior (in some weak sense)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle=H_{\textup{in}}(v^{\alpha},v).

(1.17)

We can thus assume formally that solutions to (
1.16
) converge to a Dirac measure. More precisely, let us denote the limit of
H_{\epsilon}
(in whichever sense and if it exists) by
H
and that
H(y,v,t)=G(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
. By the computations above, we obtain that
G
can be expected to satisfy a coagulation equation in which the coagulation kernel is a kernel of homogeneity
\gamma-\alpha<1
for which existence is well-known.

It is also worthwhile to mention that despite the fact that one-dimensional coagulation equations with a diagonal collision kernel have already been the subject of study in the mathematical literature
[
26
,
23
,
24
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
, a motivation of how this type of kernels could appear has not yet been made. In addition, we remember that at the moment there are only few coagulation kernels for which the long-time behavior of solutions is known. These kernels are the constant kernel, the product kernel
K(v,w)=vw
, the sum kernel
K(v,w)=v+w
, the diagonal kernel, and perturbations of the constant kernel of the form
K(v,w)=2+\epsilon W(v,w)
, with
W(v,w)
continuous, symmetric, bounded, and homogeneous of degree zero. The long-time behavior for general classes of kernels for coagulation equations remains an open problem. We refer to
[
25
]
for the first three kernels, to
[
24
,
26
,
23
]
for the diagonal kernel, and to
[
7
,
30
]
for perturbations of the constant kernel. We thus obtain a first physical intuition on how coagulation models with a diagonal kernel could emerge: as the limiting behavior of coagulation models describing the sedimentation of particles (
1.4
) in the case of very fast sedimentation speed.

We conjecture that this limiting behavior can be proved rigorously. In the present paper, we rigorously prove that the sequence
\{H_{\epsilon}\}
in (
1.16
) converges to
H
as in (
1.9
) as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. The fact that
G
in (
1.9
) satisfies (
1.11
) will be the study of a future work. Nonetheless, as noticed in Remark
1.4
, there is no result in the present literature proving that there is a uniform time (in
\epsilon
) of existence of mass-conserving solutions for (
1.16
). The first step for proving that
H_{\epsilon}
converges to
H
as in (
1.9
), with
G
satisfying (
1.11
), is thus to prove existence of mass-conserving solutions of (
1.16
) for times of order one. This result is also included in the current work.

1.5
Connection with coagulation models with fast fusion

It is also worth to analyze the similarities of the model (
1.4
) with coagulation models with fusion.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}P_{\epsilon}(a,v,t)+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{a}[(c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}}-a)P_{\epsilon}(a,v,t)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(0,a)}\int_{(0,v)}K(a-a^{\prime},v-v^{\prime},a^{\prime},v^{\prime})P_{\epsilon}(a-a^{\prime},v-v^{\prime},t)P_{\epsilon}(a^{\prime},v^{\prime},t)\textup{d}v^{\prime}\textup{d}a^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(0,\infty)}K(a,v,a^{\prime},v^{\prime})P_{\epsilon}(a,v,t)P_{\epsilon}(a^{\prime},v^{\prime},t)\textup{d}v^{\prime}\textup{d}a^{\prime}.

(1.18)

The additional variable
a
represents in this case the total surface area of the cluster and the transport term
\partial_{a}[(c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}}-a)P_{\epsilon}(a,v,t)]
indicates an evolution of the particles towards a spherical shape. The term
c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}}-a
decreases the surface area of the clusters as long as it is larger than that of a sphere
c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}}
. Physically, the transport term describes the merging of two colliding particles up to when they become a sphere and it is hence termed
fusion
.

It was proved in
[
11
]
that for the model (
1.18
) we have that the sequence
\{P_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
converges to
\overline{P}=P(v,t)\delta(a-c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}})
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
,
with
P
satisfying the weak formulation of the standard one-dimensional coagulation equation

\displaystyle\int_{(0,\infty)}

\displaystyle P(v,t)\varphi(v)\textup{d}v-\int_{(0,\infty)}P(v,0)\varphi(v)\textup{d}v

(1.19)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{(0,\infty)}K(c_{0}v^{\frac{2}{3}},v,c_{0}v^{\prime\frac{2}{3}},v^{\prime})P(v,s)P(v^{\prime},s)[\varphi(v+v^{\prime})-\varphi(v)-\varphi(v^{\prime})]\textup{d}v^{\prime}\textup{d}v\textup{d}s,

for all appropriately chosen test functions
\varphi
.
A clear similarity can be noticed between the model in (
1.16
) and the equation (
1.18
). However, the spatial inhomogeneity of the model (
1.16
) justifies the presence of the diagonal kernel in the limit (
1.11
), as opposed to the model in (
1.19
). More precisely,
assuming that the limit of
H_{\epsilon}
, with
H_{\epsilon}
as in (
1.16
), behaves indeed like a Dirac measure in the space variable, this implies that as we pass to the limit as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
in (
1.16
), only particles having the same size can interact due to the space inhomogeneity of the model.

Moreover, the same methods as in
[
11
]
cannot be
used to prove the result of this paper. One issue stems from the fact that our results in relation to the model (
1.18
) are valid for Radon measure solutions. While it is possible to define Radon measure solutions for the model (
1.18
), we need
G
to be continuous in (
1.11
) in order to rigorously pass to the limit as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
in (
1.16
). Nonetheless, comparing the two results offers a clear overview into the understanding of general existence theory for coagulation models. If we work with a coagulation kernel of form
K(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\gamma}+v^{\prime\gamma}
,
\gamma>1
, in (
1.18
), we obtain in the limit a coagulation kernel of the same homogeneity. This implies that, since solutions do not exist for the model (
1.19
) when
K(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\gamma}+v^{\prime\gamma}
, in (
1.18
), we cannot expect solutions of (
1.18
) to exist in that case (unless we choose a stronger fusion speed). On the other hand, when we choose a coagulation kernel of the form
K(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\gamma}+v^{\prime\gamma}
,
1<\gamma<1+\alpha
, in (
1.16
), we obtain in the limit (
1.11
) a coagulation kernel of homogeneity
\gamma-\alpha<1
as noticed in Remark
1.2
. We can thus expect solutions to exist and to behave like a product between a Dirac measure and a function in the volume (or a mollified version of this).

This clearly establishes the following point. The contribution given by a transport term does not suffice to assure existence of mass-conserving solutions of coagulation models (in the case where existence of solutions is not known in the standard case). Rather, both the presence of a transport term and a spatially inhomogeneous model are needed.

2
Local in time existence

2.1
Setting and main results

Assumption 2.1

(Assumptions on the initial data)
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Assume that
H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
and that there exists some constant
A>0
such that

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{A}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}},

(2.1)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
v\in(0,\infty)
, and for sufficiently large
m,b>2
.

Assumption 2.2

(Assumptions on the coagulation kernel)
.

We work with a
coagulation kernel

\displaystyle 0\leq K(v,v^{\prime})=K(v^{\prime},v)\leq K_{0}(v^{\gamma}+v^{\prime\gamma}),\quad 1<\gamma<1+\alpha,

(2.2)

such that

\displaystyle K(v-v^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq K(v,v^{\prime}),\textup{ for all }0\leq v^{\prime}\leq\frac{v}{2}.

(2.3)

Remark 2.3
.

Condition (
2.3
) is a rather standard assumption in the study of coagulation equations, see for example
[
22
]
, and most of the kernels used in applications satisfy this condition. Notice that Assumption
2.2
implies that our results hold for sum-type kernels of homogeneity
\gamma>1
which are relevant since there are no solutions to the standard coagulation model for these type of kernels. Moreover, Assumption
2.2
is also valid for sum-type kernels which vanish on the diagonal, such as (
1.3
), which are used in the physical literature to describe droplet formation and the sedimentation of particles. We refer to
[
20
]
for more details on how these kernels are derived.

Definition 2.4

(Mild solutions)
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\gamma\in[0,1+\alpha)
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, sufficiently large. Let
T>0
and
K
satisfy Assumption
2.2
. We say that a non-negative function
H_{\epsilon}\in\textup{C}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)))

is a mild solution of equation (
1.16
) if

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)-S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{\epsilon}(y,v,0)]D[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,0,t)=

(2.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,v)}D[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,s,t)S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[K(v-v^{\prime},v^{\prime})H_{\epsilon}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{\epsilon}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s,

for all
t\in[0,T]
,
v\in(0,\infty)
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, where

\displaystyle D[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,s,t):=\textup{e}^{-\int_{s}^{t}a[H_{\epsilon}](e^{\frac{t-\tau}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha},v,\tau)\textup{d}\tau},

(2.5)

with

\displaystyle a[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,t):=\int_{(0,\infty)}K(v,v^{\prime})H_{\epsilon}(y,v^{\prime},t)\textup{d}v^{\prime},

(2.6)

and

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}(s)H_{\epsilon}(y,v):=e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}H_{\epsilon}(e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha},v).

(2.7)

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. We call
H_{\epsilon}
a mass-conserving solution of equation (
1.16
) if
H_{\epsilon}
is as in Definition
2.4
and satisfies in addition

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}vH_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}vH_{\epsilon}(y,v,0)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y\textup{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Some notation.
For two quantities
x,y
, we use the notation

x\approx y
to say that there exists a constant
M>1
such that
\frac{1}{M}y\leq x\leq My
. Additionally, we denote

x\gg y
or
y\ll x
to mean that
My\leq x
, for a sufficiently large constant
M>1
.

In Subsection
3.2
, the dependence on the parameters plays an important role. We thus introduce the following notation which is valid throughout Subsection
3.2
. We write
C
for a numerical constant that can change from line to line but which does not depend on the parameters
\alpha,\gamma,b,m
, where
\alpha,\gamma,b,m
are as in Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.2
. In addition, we write
C(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
for a constant that can change from line to line which depends on the parameters
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
.

In Subsection
2.2
and in Subsection
2.3
, we offer some formal arguments to justify the proofs in this paper. For this purpose, for two quantities
x
and
y
, we introduce the notation
x\sim y
to indicate that
x
can be approximated at a formal level by
y
. This notation will be used in Subsection
2.2
and in Subsection
2.3
.

Let
A\geq 1
. Let
j\in\{1,2\}
. We denote by

\displaystyle M_{j}

\displaystyle:=M_{j}(\alpha,\gamma,b,m),\textup{ a sufficiently large but fixed constant which depends on }\alpha,\gamma,b,m;

(2.8)

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle:=C_{0}(A),\textup{ a constant such that }C_{0}^{m-1}:=M_{1}(\alpha,\gamma,b,m)A.

(2.9)

We remark that
M_{2}
is chosen as in (
4.43
) and that
M_{1}
depends on
M_{2}
through (
3.16
). We then define the functions
\psi,T_{1},T_{2},
and
T_{3}
as below.

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+|z|^{m}};

(2.10)

\displaystyle T_{1}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\chi_{1}(y,v,t);

(2.11)

\displaystyle T_{2}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2M_{1}A^{3}\epsilon}{(1+v^{b})|y-v^{\alpha}|}\chi_{2}(y,v,t);

(2.12)

\displaystyle T_{3}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{M_{2}A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\chi_{3}(y,v,t);

(2.13)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\geq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\}};

(2.14)

\displaystyle\chi_{2}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\}};

(2.15)

\displaystyle\chi_{3}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\}}.

(2.16)

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\overline{d}:=2\bigg\lceil\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1-\gamma}-1\bigg)\bigg\rceil,

(2.17)

where
\lceil\cdot\rceil
denotes the ceiling function. We then define
\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha)
to be

\displaystyle\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha)=\alpha\overline{d}+2.

(2.18)

Remark 2.6
.

Notice that

\displaystyle T_{2}(y,v,t):=\frac{2C_{0}^{m-1}A^{2}\epsilon}{(1+v^{b})|y-v^{\alpha}|}\chi_{2}(y,v,t).

The reason for introducing
C_{0}(A)
is since the cut-offs
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
in (
2.14
)-(
2.15
) (and regions included in the respective sets) will play an important role in the estimates. We refer to (
3.20
) and (
3.2
). Thus, we introduce
C_{0}
in order to simplify the notation.

The main result of this paper is as follows.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}
be as in Assumption
2.1
and
K
as in Assumption
2.2
. Let
b\geq\operatorname{max}\{\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha),

2\gamma+1\}
and
m>\operatorname{max}\{\frac{2(\gamma+1)}{\alpha},\frac{b}{\alpha}+1\}
, where
\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha)
is as in (
2.18
). There exist two constants
M_{1}(\alpha,\gamma,b,m),M_{2}(\alpha,\gamma,b,m),
depending only on
\alpha,\gamma,b,m,
such that for any
A\geq 1
, there exist
\overline{\epsilon}\in(0,1)
, sufficiently small, and
T>0
, sufficiently small, which is independent of
\epsilon\in(0,1),
such that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\overline{\epsilon}]
there exists a mass-conserving mild solution
H_{\epsilon}\in\textup{C}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)))
to (
1.16
) as in Definition
2.5
. Moreover, we have that
H_{\epsilon}
satisfies the following estimates for
C_{0}
as in (
2.9
).

If
e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}v^{\alpha}\big[1-\frac{1}{3^{\alpha}}\big]\geq C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
, then

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}(y,v,t)+T_{2}(y,v,t)+T_{3}(y,v,t).

(2.19)

Otherwise, if
e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}v^{\alpha}\big[1-\frac{1}{3^{\alpha}}\big]\leq C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
, then

\displaystyle 0\leq(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq 2Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})).

(2.20)

The bounds in (
2.19
) can be explained as follows. Since our functions
H_{\epsilon}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, approach a Dirac measure supported on the curve
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we expect that most of the mass of the solution is contained in the region
|y-v^{\alpha}|\ll 1
. Outside this region, we expect the function to have small values and to approach
0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. In other words, since
H_{\epsilon}
can be thought of as a mollifier approaching the region
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
, most of the mass of
H_{\epsilon}
will be contained in the region where
e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|\ll 1
. In other words, our choice of space is a rescaled version of the function

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y-v^{\alpha}|\leq 1\}}+\frac{\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y-v^{\alpha}|\geq 1\}}.

Remark 2.8
.

If we denote by

\displaystyle T_{\textup{min}}:=\sup_{t\geq 0}\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}vH_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}vH_{\epsilon}(y,v,0)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y\bigg\},

then
T_{\textup{min}}>0
and
T_{\textup{min}}
is independent of the choice of
\epsilon\in(0,\overline{\epsilon}),
where
\overline{\epsilon}
is as in Theorem
2.7
. In addition, the choice of
T_{\textup{min}}
and
\overline{\epsilon}
depends on the total mass of particles at initial time through the choice of
A
. For the exact choices of
T_{\textup{min}}
and
\overline{\epsilon}
, we refer to the Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Remark 2.9
.

Notice that
\overline{d}
in (
2.17
) is even and
\gamma\leq\frac{\overline{d}\alpha}{\overline{d}+1}+1
. This will be needed in Proposition
4.7
and in Proposition
4.1
.

Remark 2.10
.

As already mentioned in Remark
1.4
, Theorem
2.7
yields existence of mass-conserving solutions for times of order
\mathcal{O}(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}})
to the equation (
1.4
). We refer to Appendix
A
for more details.

We summarize below the results in
[
9
]
and Theorem
2.7
. We notice that the order of the time of existence can be improved if the upper bound of
H_{\epsilon}
contains instead the error term
T_{2}
as defined in (
2.12
). While
T_{2}(y,v,t)
is larger than
\frac{Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\chi_{2}(y,v,t)}{(1+v^{b})(1+e^{\frac{tm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})}
, it still vanishes as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
when
y
and
v
are supported away from the line
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
.

Results in
[
9
]

Theorem
2.7

existence for
t\in[0,\epsilon\ln(\tilde{T}+1)]
, with
\tilde{T}
small

existence for
t\in[0,T]
, with
T
small

H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{A}{1+|y|^{m}+v^{p}}

H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{A}{(1+v^{b})(1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})}

H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq\frac{CAe^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}}{1+|e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}|^{m}+v^{p}},
for some
C>0

H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq\frac{CAe^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}[\chi_{1}(y,v,t)+\chi_{3}(y,v,t)]}{(1+v^{b})(1+e^{\frac{tm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})}+T_{2}

Table 2
:
Results in
[
9
]
in comparison to Theorem
2.7

Remark 2.11
.

Notice that if in (
2.1
) we have that

(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{B}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}
, with
B<1
, then
(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{1}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}
and we can apply Theorem
2.7
with
A=1
in order to obtain existence of mass-conserving solutions in this case.

Actually, we can argue in a similar manner as in the case
A\geq 1
in order to obtain that if
(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{A}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}
, with
A<1
, then the following holds. For
C_{0}^{m-1}=M_{1}(\alpha,\gamma,b,m)
, if
e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}v^{\alpha}\big[1-\frac{1}{3^{\alpha}}\big]\geq C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
, then

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}(y,v,t)+T_{3}(y,v,t)+\frac{2C_{0}^{m-1}\epsilon}{(1+v^{b})|y-v^{\alpha}|}\chi_{2}(y,v,t),

(2.21)

where
T_{1},T_{3}
are as in (
2.11
) and (
2.13
). Otherwise, if
e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}v^{\alpha}\big[1-\frac{1}{3^{\alpha}}\big]\leq C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
, then

\displaystyle 0\leq(1+v^{b})H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq 2Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})).

(2.22)

Notice that this estimate provides accurate information about the distribution of mass of the solution
H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)
. The term in (
2.21
) which plays a role similar to
T_{2}
does not contain any dependence on
A
. Therefore, if
A
is very small, it yields a contribution that does not vary depending on
A
.

Nevertheless, as explained above,
T_{2}
is to be understood as an error term since it vanishes as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
when
y
and
v
are supported away from the line
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
. Thus, despite the fact that
T_{2}
loses the dependence on
A
when
A<1
, it has a small contribution. In other words, we can consider at a formal level that we stay close to the mass at initial time also in the case
A<1
.

Nonetheless, as stated in Remark
2.8
, the time of existence varies depending on
A
when
A\geq 1
. Because
T_{2}
does not depend on
A
when
A<1
, the minimal time of existence will not vary depending on
A
when
A<1
. More precisely, for
T_{\textup{min}}
as in Remark
2.8
, we have that
T_{\textup{min}}
when
A<1
is equal to
T_{\textup{min}}
when
A=1
.

As conjectured in the Introduction, we obtain that the solutions
H_{\epsilon}
found in Theorem
2.7
converge to a Dirac measure.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}
be as in Assumption
2.1
and
K
as in Assumption
2.2
. Let
T>0
and
H_{\epsilon}\in\textup{C}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)))
be as in Theorem
2.7
. Let
\sigma>0
and assume in addition that
b>\gamma+3
. There exists a subsequence (which we do not relabel) and
H\in\textup{C}([\sigma,T];\mathscr{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)))
, where we denoted by
\mathscr{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
the space of non-negative Radon measures, such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y\rightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}H(y,v,t)\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y,

as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, for every
t\in[\sigma,T]
, and every
\varphi\in\textup{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
. Moreover, for every
t\in[\sigma,T]
,
H
has the form

\displaystyle H(y,v,t)=\overline{H}(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})

(2.23)

in the sense of measures. More precisely, there exists
\overline{H}\in\textup{C}([\sigma,T];\mathscr{M}_{+}((0,\infty)))
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}H(y,v,t)\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}\overline{H}(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y,

for every
t\in[\sigma,T]
and
\varphi\in\textup{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
.

However, it is not clear whether the limit
\overline{H}
satisfies a one-dimensional coagulation equation with a diagonal kernel as in (
1.11
). From the bound in (
2.19
), we are able to prove that the sequence
\{H_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
is a uniformly bounded sequence in
L^{1}
, but it is not uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}
. This, together with the spatial inhomogeneity of the model (
1.16
), will create difficulties when trying to prove that the marginals
\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\textup{d}y
are uniformly equicontinuous in the
v
variable. As such, we do not know whether
\overline{H}
is continuous in
v
in order to rigorously derive (
1.11
).

2.2
Justification of the main results

Existence of solutions - Theorem
2.7

We first offer some insight into the proof of existence stated in Theorem
2.7
and the choice of
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
in (
2.11
)-(
2.13
). We start by approximating the contribution of the gain term in the coagulation kernel assuming that our solutions behave like Dirac measures supported on the curve
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
with sufficiently fast decay for large values of
v
. The decay in
v
is so that we obtain mass-conserving solutions. More precisely, we have that

\displaystyle v^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{v}\frac{1}{1+w^{b}}\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\frac{1}{1+(v-w)^{b}}\delta(y-(v-w)^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle\approx\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\int_{0}^{v}\delta(w-\frac{v}{2})\textup{d}w=\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}).

Applying the semigroup action to the obtained term, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\bigg]\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{t-s}{\epsilon}}\delta(e^{\frac{t-s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\delta(e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\epsilon\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}e^{s}\delta(e^{s}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\textup{d}s.

Making the change of variables
z=e^{s}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}
, we further obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\bigg]\textup{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{v^{\alpha}-y}\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\int_{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}}^{y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}}\delta(z)\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{v^{\alpha}-y}\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}-e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\}}.

This explains the choice of the term
T_{2}
in (
2.12
).

Choice of the estimate in (
2.19
)

As noticed in the previous computations the gain term of the coagulation operator shifts the support from
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
to
\{y=(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\}
if we apply it on a Dirac mass
\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
. For convenience, we approximate the term
\frac{\epsilon}{v^{\alpha}-y}\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}-e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\}}
by
\frac{\epsilon}{v^{\alpha}-y}\frac{v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(1+v^{b})^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}\}}
in the following. However, the fact that
T_{2}
is at a distance of order
v^{\alpha}e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}
from the curve
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
will be important in order to avoid unbounded terms. Because of the shift of the support, it is natural to look for a decomposition of the form
H_{\epsilon}=h_{0}+h_{1}+\ldots
, with
h_{0}(y,v,t)=g(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
and each
h_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
, supported in a region of the form
y\in[(\frac{v}{2^{i}})^{\alpha},(\frac{v}{2^{i-1}})^{\alpha})
, with a decay in
v
of larger order, and
h_{i}\approx\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{i})
. It then holds that

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}g(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})+\partial_{y}((v^{\alpha}-y)h_{1})

\displaystyle=\epsilon v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}g(v-w,t)\delta(y-(v-w)^{\alpha})g(w,t)\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle\approx\epsilon v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\bigg(g\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}).

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}((v^{\alpha}-y)h_{1})=\epsilon v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\bigg(g\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}\delta(y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}),

which implies that

\displaystyle(v^{\alpha}-y)h_{1}=\epsilon v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\bigg(g\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{y}\delta(\xi-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\textup{d}\xi

or in other words

\displaystyle h_{1}=\frac{\epsilon v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}\bigg(g\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}\}}.

Thus it holds that
h_{0}(y,v,t)=g(v,t)\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
and
h_{1}(y,v,t)=\frac{\epsilon v^{\gamma+1-\alpha}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}\bigg(g\big(\frac{v}{2},t\big)\bigg)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}\}}
. We further obtain that

\displaystyle v^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{v}h_{0}(y,v-w,t)h_{1}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\epsilon v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{v}g(v-w,t)\delta(y-(v-w)^{\alpha})w^{\gamma+1-\alpha}\frac{(g(\frac{w}{2},t))^{2}}{w^{\alpha}-y}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{w}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq w^{\alpha}\}}\textup{d}w.

Since if
y=(v-w)^{\alpha}
and
(\frac{w}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq w^{\alpha}
implies that
\frac{2v}{3}\geq w\geq\frac{v}{2}
and thus
\frac{v}{3}\leq v-w\leq\frac{v}{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{v}h_{0}(y,v-w,t)h_{1}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w\approx\frac{\epsilon v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{((v-y^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})^{\alpha}-y)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\}}.

Thus, arguing as before, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}((v^{\alpha}-y)h_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{2}v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{((v-y^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})^{\alpha}-y)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\epsilon^{2}v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}-y}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\}},

which by integrating in
y
implies that

\displaystyle h_{2}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{1}{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}-\xi}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\leq\xi\leq(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\}}.

(2.24)

The integral will give a logarithmic contribution which explodes at
y=(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}
. However, due to the cut-off in
h_{1}
noticed above, namely
\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq y\leq v^{\alpha}-e^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(v^{\alpha}-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha})\}}
, the integral turns out to be convergent. Taking into account the effect of this cut-off, we are able to estimate the integral in (
2.24
) as
C\big(\frac{t}{\epsilon}+\log(v^{\alpha})\big)
and thus

\displaystyle h_{2}\approx\frac{t\epsilon v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}v^{2(\gamma+1-\alpha)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}\log(v^{\alpha}).

Then, by choosing a sufficiently good decay in
v
for large values of
v
for the function
g(v,t)
and for times
t
of order one, we should be able to approximate
h_{2}
for
v\geq 1
by

\displaystyle h_{2}\sim\frac{\epsilon v^{2(\gamma+1)}g(\frac{v}{3},t)(g(\frac{v}{4},t))^{2}}{(v^{\alpha}-y)}.

This is the expected choice for the estimate in (
2.19
) and explains the choice of the truncation in
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in (
2.11
)-(
2.12
).

Another problem that will arise when passing to the rigorous computations is the control of the decay in
v
in order to obtain mass-conserving solutions. We remark that if the function
g
is chosen to have sufficiently fast decay for large
v
, then
h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}
also have sufficiently fast decay in
v
. However, the terms of the series become difficult to study after multiple iterations. Moreover, our functions will behave like mollifiers of a Dirac measure supported on the curve
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
and because of this their support will become larger, creating the possibility of losing the decay in
v
after their convolution. More precisely, for
\frac{y}{v^{\alpha}}\ll 1
, we may lose the decay in
v
. Nonetheless, we notice that for
\frac{y}{v^{\alpha}}\ll 1
, our equation can be approximated by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]-\mathbb{K}[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,t)

(2.25)

\displaystyle\sim\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)-\mathbb{K}[H_{\epsilon}](y,v,t).

(2.26)

For fixed
\epsilon
, existence of the model (
2.26
) has been studied in
[
9
]
. More precisely, in this case the coagulation operator can be approximated by a transport term in the
v
variable and one can prove that
G_{\epsilon}
is a supersolution of the model (
2.26
), where
G_{\epsilon}
satisfies the following PDE.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{Lv^{\gamma}}{1+|y|^{d}}\partial_{v}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)=0,

(2.27)

for some suitably chosen
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
L>0
. For completeness, we present the idea behind passing from (
2.26
) to (
2.27
) in Subsection
2.3
. This idea was also used in
[
9
, Section 2.1]
and a rigorous proof can be found in Subsection
4.4
of this paper.

Using (
2.27
), the precise form of
G_{\epsilon}
can then be studied since the associated characteristics
to (
2.27
) are given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}{Y_{\epsilon}}(y,v,t)&=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{V_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha},&\qquad{Y_{\epsilon}}(y,v,0)&=y,\\
\partial_{t}{V_{\epsilon}}(y,v,t)&=-\frac{{L}{V_{\epsilon}}^{\gamma}}{1+|{Y_{\epsilon}}|^{d}},&\qquad{V_{\epsilon}}(y,v,0)&=v.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.28)

It immediately follows from (
2.28
) that we can use separation of variables to obtain more information about the form of the characteristics since

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{d}V_{\epsilon}}{\textup{d}Y_{\epsilon}}=\frac{\epsilon LV_{\epsilon}^{\gamma-\alpha}}{1+|Y_{\epsilon}|^{d}}\Rightarrow V_{\epsilon}^{-\gamma+\alpha}\textup{d}V_{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon L\textup{d}Y_{\epsilon}}{1+|Y_{\epsilon}|^{d}}.

However, for a rigorous proof, a supersolution of (
2.25
) has the following form (up to minor modifications)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}(v^{\alpha}-y)\partial_{y}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{Lv^{\gamma}}{1+|y|^{d}}\partial_{v}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)=0.

(2.29)

The associated characteristics for (
2.29
) satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{d}V_{\epsilon}}{\textup{d}Y_{\epsilon}}=\frac{\epsilon LV_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}}{(1+|Y_{\epsilon}|^{d})(V_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}-Y_{\epsilon})}

(2.30)

and it is not possible to solve (
2.30
) using the separation of variables and implicitly we are not able to reproduce the methods in
[
9
]
. Nonetheless, we will prove that for
\frac{y}{v^{\alpha}}\ll 1
, namely for
y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
, the associated characteristics behave similarly to the ones in (
2.28
). In particular, the characteristics do not go to infinity in finite time. This explains the choice of
T_{3}
in (
2.13
).

Convergence to a Dirac measure - Theorem
2.12

We now present some justification for the proof of Theorem
2.12
. The solution found in Theorem

2.7
satisfies the following upper bound,

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}(y,v,t)+T_{2}(y,v,t)+T_{3}(y,v,t),\textup{ with }T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\textup{ as in }\eqref{def t1}-\eqref{def t3}.

(2.31)

With this upper bound, it is straightforward to prove that
H_{\epsilon}
vanishes in the region
\{|y-v^{\alpha}|\geq\delta\}
, for some
\delta>0
. More precisely, we prove that if
t>0
,
|y-v^{\alpha}|\geq\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq C(\delta)[\epsilon+e^{-\frac{t(m-1)}{\epsilon}}]\rightarrow 0\textup{ as }\epsilon\rightarrow 0.

(2.32)

If in addition to (
2.32
),
H_{\epsilon}
is also equicontinuous in time, then Theorem
2.12
follows. At a first glance, the transport term in (
1.16
) may bring a contribution that is too large because of the presence of
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
and a more detailed analysis of the equation is then required in order to prove equicontinuity in time.

Assume we are at a time
t\geq\sigma>0
. At a formal level,
H_{\epsilon}
behaves like a mollifier of a Dirac measure at
\{y=v^{\alpha}\}
with sufficiently fast decay in the
v
variable. In other words, the part of
H_{\epsilon}
supported in the same region as
T_{1}
in (
2.11
) contains most of the mass. We test with
\varphi
in (
1.16
) and integrate in
y
and
v
. It follows that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{(0,\infty)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})}{(1+v^{b})(1+e^{\frac{tm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})}\partial_{y}\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}y\textup{d}v\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{\textup{d}v}{1+v^{b}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\xi|\textup{d}\xi}{1+|\xi|^{m}}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{\sigma}{\epsilon}}.

In other words, the transport term in (
1.16
) behaves like

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(0,\infty)}(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,z)\partial_{y}\varphi(y,v)\textup{d}v\textup{d}y\textup{d}z\sim\frac{Ce^{-\frac{\sigma}{\epsilon}}|t-s|}{\epsilon}\leq C|t-s|.

for all
t,s\geq\sigma>0
. This offers a sufficiently good bound in order to obtain the desired equicontinuity in time. Moreover, the contribution due to the coagulation operator can be easily controlled using (
2.31
).

2.3
Approximation of the model by a transport term in the region
y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}

Finally, we present formally the approximation of (
2.26
) by (
2.27
) and refer to Subsection
4.4
for a rigorous proof. We can approximate (
2.26
) via

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle\sim\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}[K(v-w,w)H_{\epsilon}(y,v-w,t)-K(v,w)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]H_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w.

(2.33)

Since our strategy relies on finding a suitable supersolution, it suffices to find a lower bound for (
2.33
). We use (
2.3
), i.e.,
K(v-w,w)\leq K(v,w)
when
w\in[0,\frac{v}{2}]
, and obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle-\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}[K(v-w,w)H_{\epsilon}(y,v-w,t)-K(v,w)H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]H_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w

(2.34)

\displaystyle\geq\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)-\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}K(v,w)[H_{\epsilon}(y,v-w,t)-H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]H_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w.

Since
K(v,w)\leq K_{0}(w^{\gamma}+v^{\gamma})
and since
w\in[0,\frac{v}{2}]
, we further deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle\sim\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}v^{\gamma}[H_{\epsilon}(y,v-w,t)-H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]H_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=-v^{\gamma}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}\int_{v-w}^{v}\partial_{v}H_{\epsilon}(y,z,t)\textup{d}zH_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w.

(2.35)

Assuming that
\partial_{v}H_{\epsilon}(y,z,t)
behaves similarly for
z\in[\frac{v}{2},v]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\sim-v^{\gamma}\partial_{v}H_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}wH_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w.

(2.36)

We denote by

M_{1}(y,t):=\int_{(0,\infty)}wH_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w
the first moment in
v
of
H_{\epsilon}
. We consider only large values of
v
so that we can safely assume that
\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}wH_{\epsilon}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w
contains most of the mass. Suppose now that
M_{1}(y,t)
decays sufficiently fast for large values of
y
, that is
assume that

\displaystyle M_{1}(y,t)\leq\frac{L}{1+|y|^{d}},

(2.37)

for some sufficiently large
d
and some
L>0
. Combining (
2.37
) with (
2.36
), we obtain that
H_{\epsilon}
can be estimated formally from above by a solution of the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}v^{\alpha}\partial_{y}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{Lv^{\gamma}\partial_{v}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)}{1+|y|^{d}}=0.

(2.38)

2.4
Mathematical toolbox

The proof of Theorem
2.7
relies on the fact that for small values of
\epsilon
,
H_{\epsilon}
should behave like a mollified Dirac measure in the space variable of the form
\delta(y-v^{\alpha})
. Following the computations in (
1.2
), the gain term in the coagulation operator will behave like a Dirac measure
of the form
\delta(y-\big(\frac{v}{2}\big)^{\alpha})
multiplied by some function depending on the
v
variable. In other words, when we are at a positive distance from the line
\{y=\big(\frac{v}{2}\big)^{\alpha}\}
, we should obtain sufficiently fast decay for our solutions in a straightforward manner. The terms
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
in (
2.11
), (
2.13
) represent the action of the semigroup generated by the sedimentation term on the initial condition in (
2.1
). The term
T_{2}
in (
2.12
) is an additional term to control the problematic region approaching
\{y=\big(\frac{v}{2}\big)^{\alpha}\}
.

We gather here some technical lemmas that will be useful later in the proof of Theorem
2.7
and that provide some insight into the choice of space for existence. Firstly, we need estimates for the gain term in the coagulation operator. As noticed in Subsection
2.2
, we often use the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{v}\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\delta(y-(v-w)^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{v}\delta(y-w^{\alpha})\delta(w^{\alpha}-(v-w)^{\alpha})\textup{d}w

(2.39)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\bigg(\frac{v}{2}\bigg)^{1-\alpha}\delta\bigg(y-\Big(\frac{v}{2}\Big)^{\alpha}\bigg)\int_{0}^{v}\delta\bigg(w-\frac{v}{2}\bigg)\textup{d}w,

(2.40)

or alternatively, in (
2.39
) we use the fact that for
\xi,B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\delta(\xi-B_{1})\delta(\xi-B_{2})=\delta(B_{1}-B_{2})\delta(\xi-B_{1})=\delta(B_{1}-B_{2})\delta(\xi-B_{2}).

(2.41)

Our solutions behave like a mollified version of a Dirac measure at
y=v^{\alpha}
. While we cannot expect (
2.41
) to be valid, we need a similar estimate to the one in (
2.41
) to hold for our solutions. We prove an estimate of the form

\displaystyle H_{\epsilon}(\xi-B_{1})H_{\epsilon}(\xi-B_{2})\sim H_{\epsilon}(B_{1}-B_{2})[H_{\epsilon}(\xi-B_{1})+H_{\epsilon}(\xi-B_{2})].

More precisely, we prove that

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\xi,B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. It holds that

\displaystyle(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)\geq\frac{(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)}{2}.

(2.42)

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. As an immediate consequence of (
2.42
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)^{m}}\leq\frac{2^{2m-1}}{(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)^{m}}\bigg[\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)^{m}}+\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)^{m}}\bigg],

(2.43)

for any
\xi,B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

This will in turn imply that for
\psi
as in (
2.10
), it holds that

\displaystyle e^{\frac{2t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(\xi-B_{1}))\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(\xi-B_{2}))\leq Ce^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(B_{1}-B_{2}))\bigg[e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(\xi-B_{1}))+e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(\xi-B_{2}))\bigg].

Proof of Lemma
2.13
.

Let
\xi,B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and assume without loss of generality that
B_{1}\leq B_{2}
.

If
\xi\leq B_{1},
it holds that

\displaystyle(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)

\displaystyle\geq(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)

\displaystyle=(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|).

If
\xi\geq B_{2},
it holds that

\displaystyle(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)

\displaystyle\geq(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)

\displaystyle=(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|).

If
\xi\in[B_{1},B_{2}],
it holds that

\displaystyle(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|),

since

\displaystyle|B_{1}-B_{2}|\leq|\xi-B_{1}|+|\xi-B_{2}|\leq 2\operatorname{max}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\}.

Thus, (
2.42
) holds. Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle[(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)

\displaystyle+(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)](1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})

\displaystyle\geq(1+\operatorname{max}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})

\displaystyle=(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)

which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\}}\leq\frac{1}{1+|\xi-B_{1}|}+\frac{1}{1+|\xi-B_{2}|}.

By (
2.42
), it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)^{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{m}}{(1+\operatorname{min}\{|\xi-B_{1}|,|\xi-B_{2}|\})^{m}(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)^{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{m}}{(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)^{m}}\bigg[\frac{1}{1+|\xi-B_{1}|}+\frac{1}{1+|\xi-B_{2}|}\bigg]^{m}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{2m-1}}{(1+|B_{1}-B_{2}|)^{m}}\bigg[\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{1}|)^{m}}+\frac{1}{(1+|\xi-B_{2}|)^{m}}\bigg]

and therefore (
2.43
) holds.
∎

We also use in (
2.40
) the fact that

\displaystyle\delta((v-w)^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha})=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\Big(\frac{v}{2}\Big)^{1-\alpha}\delta\Big(w-\frac{v}{2}\Big).

Similarly, the following inequality holds for the function
\psi
in (
2.10
).

Lemma 2.14

(Change of variables)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1),\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
\beta>1
,
s\geq 0
,
y\in\mathbb{R},
and
v>0
. Then there exists a constant
C(\alpha)>0
, which depends on
\alpha
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}\frac{e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\textup{d}w}{1+e^{\frac{sn}{\epsilon}}|(v-w)^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha}|^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\alpha)v^{1-\alpha};

(2.44)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}\frac{e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\textup{d}w}{1+e^{\frac{sn}{\epsilon}}|y-(v-w)^{\alpha}|^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\alpha)v^{1-\alpha}.

(2.45)

Proof.

We only prove (
2.44
) since (
2.45
) can be proved similarly. We make the change of variables
z=e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}[(v-w)^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha}]
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{d}w}{\textup{d}z}=e^{-\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\big(-\alpha(v-w)^{\alpha-1}-\alpha w^{\alpha-1}\big)^{-1}.

Since
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
w\in\big[0,\frac{v}{2}\big]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\textup{d}w}{\textup{d}z}\bigg|\leq Ce^{-\frac{s}{\epsilon}}v^{1-\alpha}.

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}\frac{e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\textup{d}w}{1+e^{\frac{sn}{\epsilon}}|(v-w)^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha}|^{\beta}}\leq Cv^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}v^{\alpha}}\frac{\textup{d}z}{1+|z|^{\beta}}\leq Cv^{1-\alpha}.

∎

Moreover, we need to derive some estimates for the action of the semigroup generated by the transport term on the function
\psi
in (
2.10
). This will further justify the term
T_{2}
in the estimate (
2.19
).

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
s\geq 0
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
v\in(0,\infty)
, and
S_{\epsilon}
be as in (
2.7
). It holds that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\psi(y-v^{\alpha})\textup{d}s\leq\frac{C\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{\operatorname{min}\{1,e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|\}}{(1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m-1})},

(2.46)

for
\psi
as in (
2.10
).

Proof.

We make the change of variables
z=t-s
and thus (
2.46
) is equivalent to proving that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}S_{\epsilon}(z)\psi(y-v^{\alpha})\textup{d}z\leq\frac{C\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{\operatorname{min}\{1,e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|\}}{(1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m-1})}.

We make the change of variables
s=e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|
and we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}}{1+e^{\frac{mz}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}\textup{d}z=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\int_{|y-v^{\alpha}|}^{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{1}{1+|s|^{m}}\textup{d}s.

It holds that

\displaystyle\int_{|y-v^{\alpha}|}^{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{1}{1+|s|^{m}}\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|y-v^{\alpha}|}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+|s|^{m}}\textup{d}s\leq\frac{C}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m-1}};

(2.47)

\displaystyle\int_{|y-v^{\alpha}|}^{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{1}{1+|s|^{m}}\textup{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}\int_{|y-v^{\alpha}|}^{e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}\textup{d}s\leq\frac{Ce^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}\leq\frac{Ce^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m-1}}.

(2.48)

Combining the above estimates, we obtain the desired inequality.
∎

Lemma 2.16

(Monotonicity properties of the semigroup)
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in(0,\infty)
. The following properties hold.

1.

Let
S_{\epsilon}
as in (
2.7
). Assume that
f(y,v)\geq g(y,v)
for all
y\geq y_{1}
, where
y_{1}\geq v^{\alpha}
. In addition, assume that
f(y,v)\leq g(y,v)
for all
y\leq y_{2}
, where
y_{2}\leq v^{\alpha}
. It holds that

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}(s)[f(y,v)]

\displaystyle\geq S_{\epsilon}(s)[g(y,v)]\textup{ if }y\geq y_{1},\textup{ for all }s\geq 0;

(2.49)

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}(s)[f(y,v)]

\displaystyle\leq S_{\epsilon}(s)[g(y,v)]\textup{ if }y\leq y_{2},\textup{ for all }s\geq 0.

(2.50)

2.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R},v\in(0,\infty),
and
w\in[0,\frac{v}{2}]
. It holds that either
|y-w^{\alpha}|\geq|y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}|
or
|y-(v-w)^{\alpha}|\geq|y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}|
.

Proof.

1.
We only prove that (
2.50
) holds. We have to prove that
e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha}\leq y_{2}
and (
2.50
) follows. This is true since
y_{2}\leq v^{\alpha}
implies that
e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})\leq y-v^{\alpha}\leq y_{2}-v^{\alpha}
, for all
y\leq y_{2}\leq v^{\alpha}
and
s\geq 0
.

2.
Since
w\in[0,\frac{v}{2}]
, if
y\geq\big(\frac{v}{2}\big)^{\alpha}
, then
y-w^{\alpha}\geq y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\geq 0
. Otherwise, since
v-w\in[\frac{v}{2},v]
, then
y-(v-w)^{\alpha}\leq y-(\frac{v}{2})^{\alpha}\leq 0
. This proves our claim.
∎

2.5
Plan of the proof of Theorem
2.7

We define inductively a sequence of solutions that solve a linear version of our coagulation model. More precisely, let
H_{n+1}
be defined in the following manner.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{n+1}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{n+1}(y,v,t)]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}[H_{n+1},H_{n}](y,v,t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}K(v-w,w)H_{n+1}(y,v-w,t)H_{n}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}K(v,w)H_{n+1}(y,v,t)H_{n}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w,

(2.51)

with
H_{n}(y,v,0)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and such that

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{n+1}(y,v,0)=(1+v^{b})H_{n}(y,v,0)\leq\frac{A}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}},\textup{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.52)

At
n=0
, we take
H_{0}
to be

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{0}(y,v,t)=\frac{Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}}{1+e^{\frac{mt}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}

(2.53)

or in other words

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{0}+\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{0}]

\displaystyle=0;

(2.54)

\displaystyle(1+v^{b})H_{0}(y,v,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{1+|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}}.

It holds that there exists
T\leq 1
sufficiently small and independent of
\epsilon
such that there exists a solution
H_{n+1}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
which solves (
2.51
) with
H_{n+1}(y,v,0)
as in (
2.52
).

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
N>1
,
\epsilon>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
T\leq 1
, sufficiently small but independent of
\epsilon,n,
or
N
, such that if
H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
such that
\textup{supp}H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}\subseteq\{[-N^{\alpha},N^{\alpha}]\times[0,N]\}
and if
H_{n}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
is such that
\textup{supp}H_{n}(t)\subseteq\{[-N^{\alpha},N^{\alpha}]\times[0,N]\}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
, then there exists
H_{n+1}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
that solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{n+1}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)H_{n+1}(y,v,t)]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{N}[H_{n+1},H_{n}](y,v,t)

(2.55)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{v}{2}}K_{N}(v-w,w)H_{n+1}(y,v-w,t)H_{n}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{N}(v,w)H_{n+1}(y,v,t)H_{n}(y,w,t)\textup{d}w,

for all
t\in[0,T]
, where

\displaystyle K_{N}(v,w)

\displaystyle:=K(v,w)\chi_{N}(v+w),\textup{ with }

(2.56)

\displaystyle\chi_{N}\in C([0,\infty)),

\displaystyle\quad\chi_{N}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]\;\mbox{ such that }\chi_{N}(v)=1\mbox{ if }v\leq\frac{N}{2}\mbox{ and }\chi_{N}(v)=0\mbox{ if }v\geq N.

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle\textup{supp}H_{n+1}(t)\subseteq\{[-N^{\alpha},N^{\alpha}]\times[0,N]\},\textup{ for all }t\in[0,T].

(2.57)

We prove Proposition
2.17
in Appendix
B
.

Remark 2.18
.

In principle, since the functions in Proposition
2.17
depend on the iteration
n
and on the truncation
N
, we have to keep in mind that we work with a sequence
H_{n,N}
. The compact support of functions will be used in Proposition
3.1
. We will then obtain a uniform fast decaying bound for the sequence of solutions
H_{n,N}
, which is in particular independent of the truncation
N
in (
2.56
) and the iteration in
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then one can pass to the limit
N\to\infty
. Since this procedure is standard once one has the bounds on the solution, we omit the dependence on
N
and work directly with
K
and equation (
2.51
) in order to simplify the notation.

We now focus on proving Theorem
2.7
.

Plan of the proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, fixed. We will prove that there exists
T\leq 1
which is independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
, it holds that if

\displaystyle H_{n}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}(y,v,t)+T_{2}(y,v,t)+T_{3}(y,v,t),\textup{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R},v\in(0,\infty),

where
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
are as in (
2.11
)-(
2.13
), then

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}(y,v,t)+T_{2}(y,v,t)+T_{3}(y,v,t),\textup{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R},v\in(0,\infty).

(2.58)

•

Step
1.
Region
y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
.

We will prove that in this region
G_{\epsilon}
is a supersolution of (
2.51
), where
G_{\epsilon}
is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)]+\frac{L\xi(v)v^{\gamma}}{1+|y|^{d}}\partial_{v}G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)=0,

for some suitably chosen
d\in\mathbb{N}
, some sufficiently large
L>1
, and where
\xi
is such that

\xi\in C([0,\infty))\,,\quad\xi\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]\;\mbox{ such that }\xi(v)=1\mbox{ if }v\geq 1\mbox{ and }\xi(v)=0\mbox{ if }v\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(2.59)

We then prove that when
y\leq\big(\frac{v}{3}\big)^{\alpha}
, it holds that
G_{\epsilon}
satisfies the following upper bound

\displaystyle G_{\epsilon}(y,v,t)\leq T_{3}(y,v,t),

(2.60)

where
T_{3}
is as in (
2.13
).

This is the content of Section
4
.

•

Step
2.
Region
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
.

Continuity argument:
We first prove by continuity that if at time
s\geq 0
, we have that
H_{n+1}(y,v,s)\leq T_{1}(y,v,s)+T_{2}(y,v,s)+T_{3}(y,v,s)
, then for a sufficiently small
\tilde{\delta}
(which depends on
\epsilon
) it holds that
H_{n+1}(y,v,z)

\leq 4[T_{1}(y,v,z)+T_{2}(y,v,z)+T_{3}(y,v,z)]
, for all
z\in[s,s+\tilde{\delta}]
. This is the content of Proposition
3.1
.

Iteration argument:
We then work with the mild formulation of the linear version of our coagulation model (
2.51
). More precisely, we have that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)=S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{n+1}(y,v,0)]D[H_{n}](y,v,0,t)

(2.61)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}D[H_{n}](y,v,s,t)S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[K(v-v^{\prime},v^{\prime})H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s,

with
D[H_{n}]
defined as in (
2.5
). In other words, we have that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{n+1}(y,v,0)]+2K_{0}v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s.

For
t\leq s+\tilde{\delta}
, the following holds.

•

By induction, we have that
H_{n}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}
.

•

By the continuity argument, we have that
H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq 4(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3})
.

We then prove that as long as
t\leq 1
and
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
, it holds that

\displaystyle Cv^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s\leq\frac{T_{1}+T_{2}}{2}.

Since if
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
we also have that
S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{n+1}(y,v,0)]\leq\frac{T_{1}+T_{2}}{2}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}+T_{2},\textup{ for all }t\leq\operatorname{min}\{1,s+\tilde{\delta}\},y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}.

(2.62)

From Step
1
, we have

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)

\displaystyle\leq T_{3},\textup{ for all }t\leq 1,y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}.

We can thus conclude that
H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},\textup{ for all }t\leq\operatorname{min}\{1,s+\tilde{\delta}\}
and then iterate the argument in order to obtain that the upper bound (
2.62
) holds true for all
t\leq 1
.

This is the content of Section
3
of this paper.

•

Step
3.
Limit as
n\rightarrow\infty

Let
t\geq 0
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
v>0
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by

\displaystyle R_{n}(y,v,t):=H_{n+1}(y,v,t)-H_{n}(y,v,t).

(2.63)

From (
2.51
), we find that
R_{n}
satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}R_{n}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)R_{n}(y,v,t)]&=\mathbb{K}[R_{n},H_{n}]+\mathbb{K}[H_{n},R_{n-1}];\\
R_{n}(y,v,0)&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(2.64)

where
\mathbb{K}[f,g]
, for two functions
f,g
, is as in (
2.51
). Since
\mathbb{K}[R_{n},H_{n}]
is a linear operator in
R_{n}
and
\mathbb{K}[H_{n},R_{n-1}]
does not depend on
H_{n}
, we use Duhamel’s principle in order to deduce that in order to find estimates for
R_{n}
it is sufficient to find estimates for the system

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}R^{s}_{n}(x,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)R^{s}_{n}(y,v,t)]&=\mathbb{K}[R^{s}_{n},H_{n}],\textup{ for }t>s;\\
R^{s}_{n}(y,v,s)&=\mathbb{K}[H_{n},R^{s}_{n-1}].\end{aligned}\right.

(2.65)

We then use induction in order to prove that

\displaystyle|R_{n}(t)|\leq(Cte^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}})^{n},\textup{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.66)

We can thus conclude that

\displaystyle\textup{ for all }t\approx\mathcal{O}(e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}),R_{n}(t)\rightarrow 0\textup{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

(2.67)

This was also the strategy in
[
9
]
. Nonetheless, the difference is that functions in
[
9
]
were uniformly bounded in
\epsilon
in time, while in our case
H_{n}(t)\leq Ce^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}
, which is the reason why the exponential term appears in (
2.66
). We then wish to extend (
2.67
) to hold for times of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
. By (
2.66
), we have that
R_{n}\big(\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\big)\leq 2^{-n}
and we can use the invariance under translations in time in order to iterate the argument. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{n}(t)=R_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg).

\tilde{R}_{n}
then satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,t)]&=\mathbb{K}\bigg[\tilde{R}_{n},H_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg)\bigg]+\mathbb{K}\bigg[H_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg),\tilde{R}_{n-1}\bigg];\\
\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,0)&=R_{n}\bigg(\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg).\end{aligned}\right.

(2.68)

Since
R_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg)\leq 2^{-n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then (
2.68
) is to be understood at a formal level as

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,t)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\partial_{y}[(v^{\alpha}-y)\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,t)]&=\mathbb{K}\bigg[\tilde{R}_{n},H_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg)\bigg]+\mathbb{K}\bigg[H_{n}\bigg(t+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}}}{2}\bigg),\tilde{R}_{n-1}\bigg];\\
\tilde{R}_{n}(y,v,0)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.69)

We are able to use again Duhamel’s principle and find suitable estimates that allow us to extend the argument to hold for longer times. We refer to Section
5
for a rigorous proof.

The choice of presentation, namely we present the proof of Step
2
before Step
1
, is since the region
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
contains the part of the solution where most of the mass will concentrate and is thus more difficult to prove.
∎

3
Region
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}

3.1
Continuity argument

We first prove the continuity argument.

Proposition 3.1

(Continuity argument)
.

Assume that at time
s\geq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,s)\leq T_{1}(y,v,s)+T_{2}(y,v,s)+T_{3}(y,v,s),

(3.1)

where
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
are as in (
2.11
)-(
2.13
). Then there exists
\tilde{\delta}
, which may depend on
n
,
\epsilon
, but is independent of
s,

y,v
, such that for all
z\in[s,\operatorname{min}\{s+\frac{\epsilon}{m},1,s+\tilde{\delta}\}]
, where
m
is as in (
2.1
), it holds that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,z)\leq 4\big[T_{1}(y,v,z)+T_{2}(y,v,z)+T_{3}(y,v,z)\big].

(3.2)

Remark 3.2
.

We will need in the proof of Proposition
3.3
that
\tilde{\delta}
is independent of
y,v
. By Proposition
2.17
, it holds that
H_{n+1}
has compact support and this will imply that
\tilde{\delta}
does not depend on
y
or
v
. We refer to Remark
2.18
. We remember we work with a sequence
H_{n,N}
, which is compactly supported and the support depends on
N
. We will then obtain a uniform fast decaying bound for the sequence of solutions
H_{n,N}
, which is in particular independent of the truncation
N
and then one can pass to the limit as
N\to\infty
. The rest of the estimates in this paper do not depend on the truncation
N
and thus do not depend on the compact support. As mentioned in Remark
2.18
, we omit the dependence on
N
in order to avoid complicated notation.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We have that (
3.1
) holds. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}:=\operatorname{min}\{\frac{A\psi(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))}{1+v^{b}},\frac{A\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|(1+v^{b})}\}.

(3.3)

Since
H_{n+1}
has compact support, it holds that there exists
\epsilon_{N}
, where
N
is as in (
2.56
), which depends on
N
but is otherwise independent on
y,v
, such that
\tilde{\epsilon}\geq\epsilon_{N}
. By continuity in time of
H_{n+1}
, we have that there exists
\tilde{\delta}
such that for all
z\in[s,s+\tilde{\delta}]
it holds that
|H_{n+1}(y,v,z)-H_{n+1}(y,v,s)|\leq\epsilon_{N}
. It thus holds that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,z)\leq T_{1}(y,v,s)+T_{2}(y,v,s)+T_{3}(y,v,s)+\epsilon_{N}.

Take
z\in[s,\operatorname{min}\{1,s+\frac{\epsilon}{m},s+\tilde{\delta}\}]
. We analyze each term separately.

\displaystyle T_{1}(y,v,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{2A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\chi_{1}(y,v,s)\leq\frac{2A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{s}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\chi_{1}(y,v,s),

with
\chi_{1}
as in (
2.14
). Since
z\leq s+\frac{\epsilon}{m}
, it holds that

\displaystyle 1+e^{\frac{zm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}=1+e^{\frac{sm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m}e^{\frac{(z-s)m}{\epsilon}}\leq 3(1+e^{\frac{sm}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})

(3.4)

and thus we further have that

\displaystyle T_{1}(y,v,s)\leq\frac{6A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\chi_{1}(y,v,s).

We define the set

\displaystyle U(s):=\{y\leq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\}.

Since
z\geq s
, we have
v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{s}{\epsilon}}\leq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{z}{\epsilon}}
. Thus

\displaystyle T_{1}(y,v,s)\leq 3T_{1}(y,v,z)+\frac{6A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)},\textup{ for }s\leq z.

(3.5)

However, in the region where
\{y\leq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\}
, it holds that
e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|\geq C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
. Thus

\displaystyle e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\frac{1}{e^{\frac{z(m-1)}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)}\leq\frac{C_{0}^{m-1}\epsilon}{|y-v^{\alpha}|}\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)}

from which we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{6A}{1+v^{b}}e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)}\leq\frac{6AC_{0}^{m-1}\epsilon}{(1+v^{b})|y-v^{\alpha}|}\mathbbm{1}_{U(z)\setminus U(s)}\leq 3T_{2}(y,v,z).

(3.6)

From (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), we deduce that

\displaystyle T_{1}(y,v,s)\leq 3[T_{1}(y,v,z)+T_{2}(y,v,z)],\textup{ }s\leq z.

Moreover, since
z\geq s
, we also have that

\displaystyle T_{2}(y,v,s)=\frac{2C_{0}^{m-1}A^{2}\epsilon}{(1+v^{b})|y-v^{\alpha}|}\chi_{2}(y,v,s)\leq T_{2}(y,v,z),\textup{ }s\leq z,

where
\chi_{2}
is as in (
2.15
). Using (
3.4
), we also have that
T_{3}(y,v,s)\leq 3T_{3}(y,v,z)
. We now find an upper bound for
\epsilon_{N}
. Let
\tilde{\epsilon}
be as in (
3.3
). We have that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N}\leq\tilde{\epsilon}\leq\frac{1}{1+v^{b}}\psi(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\geq v^{\alpha}-C_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}e^{-\frac{z}{\epsilon}}\}\cup\{y\leq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}\}}+T_{2}(y,v,z)

and moreover since
z\leq 1
it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{A}{1+v^{b}}\psi(e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))\leq\frac{e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}}{1+v^{b}}\psi(e^{\frac{z}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})).

In other words,

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}\leq T_{1}(y,v,z)+T_{2}(y,v,z)+T_{3}(y,v,z).

Thus, we have that (
3.2
) holds for all
z\in[s,\operatorname{min}\{s+\epsilon,s+\tilde{\delta},1\}],
. This concludes our proof.
∎

3.2
Iteration argument

We are now able to find an upper bound for
H_{n+1}
in the region
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
.

Proposition 3.3

(Region
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
)
.

There exist
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
and
T(A)\in[0,1]
, sufficiently small and depending on
A
in (
2.11
), such that for all
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following statement holds. Let
H_{n}
be as in (
2.51
). Let
T_{1}
,
T_{2},T_{3}
as in (
2.11
)-(
2.13
) with
b\geq\operatorname{max}\{\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha),\gamma+1\}
and
m>\operatorname{max}\{\frac{2(\gamma+1)}{\alpha},\frac{b}{\alpha}+1\}
, where
\overline{b}(\gamma,\alpha)
is as in (
2.18
). Assume that
H_{n}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{3}T_{i}
. Then it holds that
H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq T_{1}+T_{2},
for all
t\in[0,T(A)]
and
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
. Using the mild formulation of (
2.51
), it holds that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)=S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{n+1}(y,v,0)]D[H_{n}](y,v,0,t)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}D[H_{n}](y,v,s,t)S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[K(v-v^{\prime},v^{\prime})H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s,

with
D[H_{n}]
defined as in (
2.5
). Since
D[H_{n}]\leq 1
and
K(v-w,w)\leq 2K_{0}v^{\gamma}
when
w\in[0,\frac{v}{2}]
, we further have that

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(y,v,t)\leq S_{\epsilon}(t)[H_{n+1}(y,v,0)]+2K_{0}v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s.

We prove that if
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
, then

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}(t)H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)\leq\frac{T_{1}+T_{2}}{2}

(3.7)

and

\displaystyle 2K_{0}v^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(0,\frac{v}{2})}S_{\epsilon}(t-s)\bigg[H_{n+1}(y,v-v^{\prime},s)H_{n}(y,v^{\prime},s)\bigg]\textup{d}s\leq\frac{T_{1}+T_{2}}{2}.

(3.8)

We then conclude that
H_{n+1}\leq T_{1}+T_{2}
when
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
as desired. We first prove (
3.7
). We thus bound
S_{\epsilon}(t)H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)
when
y\geq(\frac{v}{3})^{\alpha}
. From (
2.1
), it holds that

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}(t)H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(y,v)=e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}H_{\epsilon,\textup{in}}(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha})+v^{\alpha},v)\leq\frac{Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}}{(1+v^{b})(1+e^{\frac{mt}{\epsilon}}|y-v^{\alpha}|^{m})}=\frac{Ae^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}\psi(e^{\frac{t}{\epsilon}}(y-v^{\alpha}))}{1+v^{b}}.

On the one hand,
